- {{user}} can also **connect a service** (Cloudflare) by making a token on its site and pasting it into Claktory; the vault keeps the connection, which no agent ever gets. An agent is given a secret made from it with only the access its work needs, and uses it as any other.
<!-- admin: ask-each-time | A secret that asks the person each time: every command using it waits for their Allow, for that exact command, once. -->
- A secret can **ask each time**: then every command that names it waits for {{user}} to allow it, in a request in the agent's conversation showing the exact command (and whether it keeps running after it starts). Allowed, that command gets it once; a different command, the same one again, or the same one in the background asks again. No app can use such a secret, and it can't be set on a connected service itself, only on secrets made from it. Turning it on is free; turning it off needs the vault open, as giving a secret does. The setting is sealed with the secret's key, so changing it in the vault's files makes the secret unusable rather than free. For the same reason a Claktory older than Ask each time (one an update went back to) can't open such a secret, and says the vault was changed outside Claktory: nothing is wrong, the secret was made by a newer Claktory, and it opens again after you update.
<!-- admin: browser | Claktory's browser: each browsing agent's own Chrome, any https site, logins Claktory types from Secrets, and what only the person may do. -->
- **Claktory's browser** is Google Chrome that Claktory runs for agents, apart from {{user}}'s own: on Linux as a user of its own (`claktory-browser`) inside a fence, once a setup command has run; on a Mac with Apple silicon, macOS 26 or later and Apple's container tool, in a small virtual machine whose image Claktory downloads and checks itself. The first time an agent needs it and it isn't set up, its tool says what's missing and the agent asks {{user}} to run the setup command in a request (Execute), since it installs something on the computer. Every agent with the **Browser** extension on has a browser of its own: its own profile, so its sign-ins are its alone, started when it opens it and closed after 10 minutes unused (its sign-ins stay); when this computer hasn't the free memory for another (each takes about 2 GB, and Claktory's browsers together never more than half its memory), it doesn't open, and the agent is told why; turning Browser off for the agent, or deleting the agent, closes it and deletes its profile, while its logins stay in Secrets. Its pages go to any https site on the internet, never this computer or the local network (over IPv4 or IPv6), never a page Chrome's Safe Browsing warns about, and never a web password manager (Google Passwords, 1Password, Bitwarden, LastPass, Dashlane, Keeper, Proton Pass, NordPass). Its Chrome has a policy of its own: no signing in to Chrome itself, no sync, no saved passwords, no third-party cookies. On a Mac, once it's set up, agents' commands can't use Apple's container tool or read its folder, where the browser's VM keeps its sessions, and their file tools can't read that folder either: that keeps the browser out of what an agent does, not out of reach of a program deliberately breaking in as {{user}}. What an agent reads on a page is a stranger's words, and marks its conversation as having read something from outside, as web_fetch does.
<!-- admin: browser-sign-in | How an agent signs in to a site in its browser with browser_sign_in: Claktory types the login; the agent never sees it. -->
- **Signing in:** an agent signs in with `browser_sign_in` on a site's sign-in form. Claktory types the login itself, at a person's pace, only on the site Chrome reports the page is on; the agent never sees it, and it's never given to a command unless {{user}} gives it to the agent in Settings → Secrets. The first time an agent needs a login for a site, {{user}} gets a request naming the site as Claktory read it ("Yukako asks to sign in to **x.com**"), with fields for the email or username and the password, saved in Secrets as that site's login and filled from then on without asking. Claktory fills {{user}}'s logins into the page itself. They never appear in the agent's conversation, its tools or its commands, and they fill only on the site they belong to. Agents still run as {{user}} on their computer, so this keeps their passwords out of what an agent sees and does, not out of reach of a program deliberately breaking in as them.
<!-- admin: browser-person-only | What only the person may do in an agent's browser (codes, terms, recovery codes, two-step, payments) and how the agent waits. -->
- **What only {{user}} may do** reaches them as a request in the agent's conversation, and the agent's browser waits until they answer: a code sent to them, terms to agree to, recovery codes to keep, turning on two-step sign-in, and a payment. However they answer, the browser is the agent's again and it's told how it went, with the page as it is then. A puzzle or a check that the agent is a person, a passkey or a sign-in link sent by email reach {{user}} as no request: Claktory tries a check again by itself a few times, and otherwise the agent says in its reply that it couldn't get past it.
<!-- admin: browser-settings | Settings → Claktory's browser: setup state, each agent's signed-in sites, Sign out, Delete browser data, Turn off browser, time zone. -->
- **Settings → Claktory's browser** shows whether it's set up on this computer (**Ready** with Chrome's version; **Not set up yet**, with **Set it up yourself** folding out the setup command; or why this computer can't run it), then a row for each agent with Browser on, saying which sites its browser is signed in to, with **⋯**: **Sign out of** each site (at once), **Delete browser data…** and **Turn off browser…** (each asks first). Then **Time zone and languages**, what every agent's browser shows sites (this device's by default, and it says so while they match), with **Change…**, and the **Browser log**: each step an agent took in its browser over the last 7 days, never what it typed, for all agents or one.
<!-- admin: browser-agent-settings | Browser's switch in Agent settings: its line, who can't have it, and what turning it on or off does. -->
- **In Agent settings**, Browser is an ordinary extension switch, with the line "Lets <agent> use websites in Claktory's browser, signing in with your logins from Secrets." For an admin agent, or one limited to a pipe or to reading reports, it's off and says why ("Admin agents can't use the browser."). Turned on, it's saved with **Save**; turned off, **Save** first asks "Turn off <agent>'s browser?", naming the sites it will be signed out of, since its browser's data is deleted (its logins stay in Secrets), and **Cancel** there keeps the form as it was. On Windows, the switch can turn the browser off but never on, since giving an agent the browser is {{user}}'s alone: it says to use a phone, tablet or Mac.
<!-- admin: browser-payment | A payment an agent's browser meets: the request, the page opened in the person's own browser, I've paid and Can't do this. -->
- When an agent reaches a **payment**, it hands it over with `browser_handoff`, kind `payment`, on the payment page. The request is "<agent> asks you to pay on <site>": the site is the page's registrable domain as Chrome reports it ("pay.stripe.com" reads stripe.com), the agent's line under it in quotes, since what it's for is the agent's claim. **Open on this Mac** (this iPhone, this iPad, this phone) opens the page's address, https only and as Chrome reported it, as a plain link in {{user}}'s own browser on that device (on an iPhone or iPad in Safari inside the app, with Apple Pay; on Android in a Chrome tab), with nothing of the agent's (no cookies, no sign-ins). Once it's opened, on any device, the request says "Opened in your browser. Come back here once you've paid." with **I've paid**, which tells the agent, and it checks the page and carries on, and **Open again**. **Can't do this…** tells the agent why it can't be paid (Not now, I don't want to pay this, or {{user}}'s own words). Ended, it reads "Paid on stripe.com". One payment request waits at a time per agent, never for a page Safe Browsing warns about. A checkout that only works inside the agent's browser (a card form built into the site) can't be paid this way: the agent says so.
<!-- admin: secret-sites | Website logins: Claktory types them only on their own site; a secret's sites; fill-only grants, which no command gets. -->
- A **website login** is a secret (USERNAME and PASSWORD) with **sites**: the exact https addresses (`https://x.com`, never a pattern) where Claktory types it. `browser_sign_in` names the page's username and password fields; when the agent holds a login for exactly the page's site as Chrome reports it, Claktory clicks into each field and types it with the browser's own keys at a person's pace, then presses Enter, and reads what the site does next for the login request; without one, {{user}} gets the login request. Claktory types only in the page itself (never a field in a frame), never on a page Chrome warns about or a password manager's site, and it checks before and after every key that the field still has the keyboard on that site: if the page moves it, it stops, empties the field and says only that. While a login request waits, nothing is typed there. A secret with no sites, or one that asks each time, is typed nowhere. A login {{user}} gives in a login request is given to the agent **to fill only**: no command or app ever gets it. A website login is never given for commands on a request, by {{user}} on its card or by an admin agent, nor offered there as the secret that fits: only giving it to an agent in Settings → Secrets lets its commands have it. Fill only keeps the value from the agent and its commands, not from the site: once typed, it's in that site's page like anything typed there, so the site has it (and anything running in its pages), and a page that shows it back (an account page that prints the email, say) shows it to the agent too, since the agent reads a page's text, though never a field's value. Each sign-in shows in the secret's Recent use and in the browser log, with where, never the value. The sites and the fill-only setting are sealed with the vault's key, so changing them in the vault's files makes them count for nothing.
<!-- admin: browser-login | A site's login asked for from the agent's browser: the login request, Sign in or Use it, and how it went. -->
- When an agent's browser meets a site's sign-in, the agent asks for the login and the request is the **login request**: "<agent> asks to sign in to **x.com**", the site as Claktory reads it from the page (its registrable domain, never the agent's words), the agent's reason, then **Email or username** and **Password** (marked as such, so an iPhone's Passwords or Android's password manager offers the site's saved login), "Saved as X login · Edit" (Edit changes the name only) and **Dismiss** · **Sign in**, which can be pressed once both are in. Sign in saves the login in the vault, sealed to that site, gives it to the agent to fill in only, and fills it in; the request then says "Signing in to x.com…", then "Signed in to x.com · Saved as X login", or "Saved as X login. x.com asks for a code next." (the code request follows). If the site refuses it, the request opens again with the password cleared, saying "x.com didn't accept it. Check it and try again." (naming the login when it's one already saved, and that signing in updates it for every agent that uses it); what was typed stays only on the device it was typed on. When one of {{user}}'s logins is for exactly that site, the request asks "Use your X login on **x.com**?" with **Use it** instead, and no fields; **Use a different login…** opens the fields for another, saved under a name of its own ("X login 2"). A page whose site isn't the one a login of the same name is for gets a warning above the fields: "This page is x-login.com, not x.com. Your X login is for x.com." The agent never sees the values.
<!-- admin: errand-codes | A code sent to the person for an agent's browser: they type it in a request and Claktory fills it into the page; the agent never sees it. -->
- When a site asks an agent's browser for a **code sent to {{user}}** (a text message, an email), the agent hands it over with `browser_handoff`, kind `code`, naming the field as its last read did (`document`, `target`). The request is Claktory's: "<site> asks for a code", the site as Chrome reports the page's origin, with one field for the code only (no comment, no attachments; on a phone, the code from Messages comes above the keyboard). Only on an https page, never on Google's, Apple's or Microsoft's sign-in, and only into a text, number or one-time code field. **Fill in** puts it into that field, only if the page and the field are still the ones the request was asked on: the agent is told it was filled, with the page as it is then, never the code, and a code that isn't one (letters and digits, at most 16) leaves the request waiting. If the page changed meanwhile, nothing is filled, the request ends saying why, and the agent reads the page again. **Can't do this** tells the agent {{user}}'s reason. The code isn't kept anywhere: not in the request, in the conversation, History, the browser log or a notification.
<!-- admin: errand-agreements | An agreement an agent's browser meets: the person reads the box's words in a request, and Accept has Claktory tick only that box. -->
- An agent never ticks an agreement in its browser itself (a box that says "I agree", "terms", "accept" or "consent"). It prepares the rest of the page, then hands it over with `browser_handoff`, kind `agreement`, naming the box (`document`, `target`). The request is Claktory's: "<site> asks you to agree", the site as Chrome reports the page's origin, with the box's words shown as the site's. **Accept** has Claktory tick only that box, and only if the page is still the one the request was asked on and the box still says the same and isn't ticked. Otherwise nothing is ticked, the request says why, and the agent reads the page again. Words typed on it go to the agent as a **reply**, and nothing is ticked. **Can't do this** tells the agent {{user}}'s reason.
<!-- admin: errand-recovery-codes | Recovery codes a site shows an agent's browser: Claktory reads them, the person saves them or keeps them in Secrets; the agent never sees them. -->
- When a site shows **recovery codes** (backup codes it shows once), an agent never reads them: its reader withholds the block and offers it only to name. The agent hands it over with `browser_handoff`, kind `recovery_codes`, naming the block (`document`, `target`), and Claktory reads the codes itself and seals them in the vault at once. The request is Claktory's, "<site> recovery codes": **Show** shows them only on the device that asks, and **Copy** puts them on a clipboard that clears itself. They stay until {{user}} chooses **I saved them**, which deletes Claktory's copy, or **Keep in Secrets**, which keeps them as a secret named "<site> recovery codes" (variable RECOVERY_CODES, one code per line), given to no agent and with no hint of its value in Settings → Secrets. The request can't be dismissed or answered with words, and it waits through restarts and the browser closing: I saved them closes it even then. While the vault is locked the codes are kept only in memory, and the request's first line says a restart deletes them; codes lost that way are gone, and I saved them closes their request. The agent's browser waits until {{user}} chooses, then the agent is told and reads the page again. The codes are never in the conversation, the browser log or Claktory's records.
<!-- admin: errand-two-step | Turning on two-step sign-in at a site in an agent's browser: the person approves it each time; codes by text message or email only. -->
- Turning on **two-step sign-in** at a site is {{user}}'s call: an agent can't press a button in its browser that turns it on until {{user}} approves a request it asks with `browser_handoff`, kind `two_step` ("<site> wants two-step sign-in turned on"). Approved, the agent may turn it on at that site while its browser stays open, with codes sent to {{user}} by text message or email (handed over as code requests). An authenticator app, a security key or a passkey is never the agent's, approved or not, and the reader never shows it a setup key or its QR code. At Google, Apple and Microsoft it's refused outright: the agent says it's {{user}}'s to set up. **Can't do this** tells the agent why.
<!-- admin: generated-keys | Keys and passwords the vault generates, and files it imports from this computer, for an agent's request, no one seeing them. -->
- An agent can ask the vault to **generate a key** (`secrets_request` with `generate: "ssh-ed25519"`): the request says what it asks for, then one line with the name (also the key's comment, in its public line), whether it's for the agent or for no one and whether it asks each time (off unless {{user}} turns it on), **Edit** opening them. {{User}} approves it themselves (no admin agent can). Claktory makes the key in the vault itself and keeps its private half as a file secret no one ever sees, given to the agent or, as it asked, to no one; the agent is told only the public half, which `secrets_requests` and the secret's description keep too. The request then shows the public key, with **Copy key** and, for an SSH key, the agent's link (**Open github.com**), to add it where the agent will use it. It can ask for a **password** the same way (`generate: "password"`, with what the service accepts: a length, the kinds of characters or the exact ones allowed, no look-alikes, at least one of each kind, the service's maximum): the request says how long it will be, what it's made of and how strong it is, and {{user}} can make it longer (or shorter, down to the shortest allowed) before **Generate**. The vault draws every character at random with the system's random numbers and never makes a weak one: at least 16 characters and 80 bits, or, for a service that takes fewer, at least 12 characters and 72 bits, at most 256; an agent asking for less is refused with the reason. The password goes in one variable (PASSWORD unless the agent names one) that no one sees, the agent included; {{user}} can read it in Settings → Secrets. Giving a secret on a request, {{user}} or an admin agent can add a note for the agent, which reaches it as its writer's ("Rivet's note: …"). An agent can also ask the vault to **import files** already on this computer (a keystore, a credentials file; `secrets_request` with `import`: a list of `{variable, path}`, at most 10): Claktory reads each file when it's asked for, and the request shows exactly which (its full path as Claktory resolved it, in full, with any invisible or reversing character escaped, its size, when it was last changed and a short fingerprint keyed with the vault's own key), never its contents, warns when a file is in a sensitive place (`~/.ssh`, `~/.gnupg`, `~/.aws`, cloud and container credentials, the Keychains, a browser's profile, `~/.netrc`) and when the asking agent has read a web page. It refuses, saying why, anything that isn't a plain file, a path with a symbolic link anywhere in it, a file with other names (hard links), a file over 1 MB, Claktory's own folders and `/dev`, `/proc` and `/sys`. {{User}} approves it themselves with **Import and give to <agent>** or **Import only** (no agent gets it until they give it to one; only this when the agent asked for it to go to no one), after one line with its settings ("Saved as … · Doesn't ask each time · Keeps the original"), whose **Edit** sets its name, whether it asks each time, and whether to delete the originals (off unless turned on; a plain delete once the secret is in the vault, and only if it's still the same file, not a secure erase). Approved, Claktory opens each file again, refuses one that changed or was replaced since it was asked for, and seals the bytes straight into a new secret, one file variable each; a file that isn't text (a .p12) is kept exactly and reaches commands byte for byte, hidden from output raw, in base64 and in hex, and Settings → Secrets shows it in base64. The agent is told the names, sizes and fingerprints, never the contents. An imported secret can be renamed, but its values can't be edited: import the file again instead. Its Recent use records the import: who asked, the files, their sizes and fingerprints, whom it was given to and whether the originals were deleted. Agents never ask {{user}} to copy a file's contents into the vault by hand; they ask to import it.

<!-- admin: app-secrets | Settings → Secrets: adding secrets and .env files, connecting services, giving and taking back, Show and Copy, recent use. -->
### Secrets in the app

- There's nothing to set up: Claktory makes the vault when it starts and opens it by itself, with a key kept on this machine that only {{user}}'s account can read. Values are encrypted on disk all the same, so stronger keys can come later without changing how secrets are used.
- Agents' requests for secrets show only in the conversation of the agent that asked, not here: give one of your secrets there, add a new one from what the agent described, or dismiss it. Giving a secret here with **Give to…** answers that agent's request for it too (one asking for that secret by name, or for the variables it sets), as giving it in the request would: the request closes and the agent carries on; given for none of its requests, the agent is told all the same, and its requests stay. Agents' suggestions to connect a service show at the top here, as in their conversations: what it's for, what it needs and why, then **Connect <service>** or **Dismiss**, with an optional reason. Connecting gives the agent nothing: Rivet, or {{user}}, gives it a secret made from the connection.
- **Connect a service…**: choose a service (Cloudflare) and **Connect**. Cloudflare's page that makes an API token opens in the browser on the device {{user}} is at, the backend's machine or another one, with what Claktory needs already chosen; there, choose the zones agents may work on and create the token, then paste it in the field Claktory shows (**Paste token**) and **Connect**. Claktory checks it with Cloudflare, keeps it with the secrets as a connection that nobody has, and never shows it again; one Cloudflare doesn't accept says so, for another to be pasted (**Open the page again** to make a new one). Only the device that started connecting can finish it. The same field shows on an agent's suggestion and for **Connect again**.
- **Add a secret…**: a name agents ask for it by, **What it can do** (exactly what it grants, such as "Cloudflare: DNS Edit on claktory.com": agents read it, and the Map and the Secrets panel show it), and one or more variables with their values (**Save as a file** for a tool that needs a key file). **Paste .env…** reads a .env file, shows the variables it found (never the values), and adds them together as one secret or as a secret for each. **Ask each time** makes it ask {{user}} before each command that uses it.
- Each secret shows its variables with the last characters of each value, who it's given to, when it was last used and by whom, and **Recent use**: each command that got it, each time it was shown, given and taken back. **Show** shows its values for 30 seconds; **Copy** copies one, and the clipboard clears after a minute. **Give to…** gives it to an agent; **Take back** takes it back, and can stop that agent's background tasks that got it. **Edit…** changes its name, description or values. **Delete…** deletes it. **Ask each time** turns asking on or off for it. **Sites** lists where Claktory's browser may fill it in when an agent signs in with it (exact https:// addresses; **Add site**, **Remove**; also when adding it), and says when it's filled in nowhere: no sites, or asking each time. **Let Claktory's browser fill it in…** gives it to an agent only for its browser to fill in on those sites, never to its commands or apps; such a grant reads "can fill it in on its sites only", and **Give to…** gives it in full again. Recent use lists each fill too ("Filled in by June on https://shop.example"). An agent's settings list the secrets it can use.
- A connection shows its service and account, what it can do and reach, and has no values: it's never given to an agent. **Make a secret from it…** makes a narrower secret to give like any other (on Cloudflare an account, any of its zones and what it may do), which Claktory renews before it expires; it says which connection it was made from. **Connect again** takes a new token in its place, keeping the secrets made from it and who has them; **Edit…** renames it; **Disconnect…** removes it and the secrets made from it (deleting their tokens at Cloudflare), and deletes the pasted token at Cloudflare too unless turned off.
- Giving, taking back, showing and deleting need no passphrase: only {{user}}'s app, signed in to this Claktory as one of their devices, can do them; agents can't give themselves a secret.
- Deleting an agent takes back every secret it had.

<!-- admin: claktory-account | Signing in to claktory.com: the first launch's step, the vault after a restart, what it sends, offline, removal. -->
### Signing in to claktory.com

- On unless `account.enabled` is false in the settings (`"account": {"enabled": false}`; `service` points it at another account service than claktory.com's, `https://` or `http://` on this computer only). While it's off, the app asks for no sign-in and Claktory sends nothing anywhere.
- The app's first step asks to sign in, and every Claktory must: a new one, one from before signing in came, and one that skipped it in an earlier version are all asked until they're signed in, and signed out, asked again. Only while claktory.com can't be reached does **Continue for now** go on without it, until the app next opens. The code step says the address the code comes from (code@auth.claktory.com), to look for in spam too. Until it's done the app behind it is dimmed and can't be used (on a Mac, only the rail of Claktories and the update banner answer); a link under the email, **How Claktory uses your email**, opens claktory.com/privacy.
- Signing in is Claktory's, not a device's: {{user}} gives an email address, then the 6-digit code emailed to it, and every device shows the same account. **Switch account…** in Settings → Account asks for another address and its code: Claktory stays signed in with the current account until that code checks out (a wrong or expired code changes nothing), and **Cancel** drops the switch. Settings → Account shows the email, the service, when it was last in touch, and exactly what was last sent. Its **Delete account…** opens the account site's step that deletes the account (it asks once more there).
- Signed in, the vault opens with the account instead of the key on this machine: after a restart, Claktory asks claktory.com for its half of the key and opens the vault by itself (the account is emailed when that looks unusual: from a network it hasn't unlocked from in the last 30 days, or as an older copy of itself; claktory.com/account lists every unlock). Updates keep the vault open without asking. While claktory.com can't be reached after a restart, Claktory works but secrets stay locked, and so do the providers' sign-ins, which are kept with them: agents that need a model wait (their rows say "Waiting to use Anthropic until your secrets unlock") and carry on by themselves once they're unlocked, and after a minute Needs you offers to unlock them with the recovery key. Signing in to a provider again works meanwhile. Claktory tries again by itself (and whenever an app connects or an agent uses a secret), or {{user}} opens them with the recovery key (this Claktory's own, even beside others on the same account: shown in Settings → Account, made at sign-in; until {{user}} confirms they saved it, by typing back one block of it that Claktory picks, Needs you keeps a quiet **Save the recovery key for <name>** item, and a new recovery key asks again): Settings → Secrets then says why they're locked, with **Unlock with recovery key…**. An account can also have one recovery key for all its Claktories, made and approved on one of {{user}}'s devices; claktory.com keeps only the half that locks, never the one that opens. When claktory.com has an account key this Claktory's secrets aren't locked to yet, its own alert in Needs you says so, and on that computer's app or a device connected and confirmed as {{user}}'s, Settings → Secrets offers **Use the key here**, only for a key whose signatures check, saying which Claktory made it and when, to use only if {{user}} made it: then the account's key opens this Claktory's secrets too (Settings → Secrets says "Covered by your account's recovery key", with the Claktory it was made on and when, and whether it was used here to open them; Settings → Account has no **Show recovery key…** once no Claktory keeps a copy of it), it's in History, where a click opens Settings → Secrets, and its own key is no longer asked about. A key that can't be checked gets an alert and nothing to choose; nothing is ever locked to a key on claktory.com's word alone. A Claktory removed at claktory.com opens only with the recovery key until it signs in again; Settings → Secrets offers **Sign in again…** and **Use recovery key…**. Signing out goes back to the key on this machine, and ends the sign-in at claktory.com too, as a removal there does: its key and token stop working for anyone holding a copy, agents lose their access to the account's apps (direct messages), its relay access and pipes' Verified run out within a week, and the next sign-in uses a new key. While claktory.com can't be reached, it's signed out here at once and Settings → Account says claktory.com will be told; Claktory tells it by itself once it can (and before signing in there again).
- What it sends, at sign-in: the email, this Claktory's id, its computer's name, version and platform, and its public key. At start and every hour: its version and platform; how many agents, projects, paired devices, pipes and active monitors it has, and roughly how many secrets (none, 1, 10 or 50 and more); whether a model has been connected and an agent has run yet; how many of its devices are each system, kind of device and app version; and, for each whole hour since the last check-in, the tokens of model calls per provider and model (with the model's name as the app shows it), how many agents worked, for how many minutes, and how many runs started, how many devices connected, and per Claktory version how often an update to it was rolled back or it stopped without stopping cleanly. Hours not yet sent (offline, say) are kept and sent later. It also registers the tunnel ids of this Claktory and its paired devices. Never sent: names of agents, projects or devices, prompts, conversations, files, secrets.

<!-- admin: notifications | Notifications: what notifies the person and when not, the settings, and pushes to phones through the relay. -->
## Notifications
- Claktory decides in its backend what notifies {{user}}, the same for every device: something new in **Needs you** (a question, a request for a secret, a proposal, a pipe message held for review, a website shared with {{user}} over a pipe, agents starting to wait on a provider's usage limit or credit: once per provider, not again while the wait lasts or when it's back within ten minutes), an agent's alert (`notify_person`), Claktory's own alert when memory, disk or processors cross a Resources threshold and no admin agent can take it, Claktory's urgent alert when its disk can't be written any more (it turned read-only, or keeps failing: once until it works again, to every device whatever the settings, quiet hours and what's in use, pushed with what was saved while the disk worked), and an agent's reply to a message {{user}} sent from a phone or tablet (from a computer too, if they turn that on). A review request (an agent's report or file for {{user}}, `ask_person` with `review`) is a question like any other: it notifies, then waits in Needs you, where a notification alone would disappear; that's why agents send what {{user}} should read as one, and keep `notify_person` for news with nothing to read. Every app connected at the time gets it, but only one whose device lets it show notifications counts as delivering it: each app says whether its device allows them (an Android app can't show them yet), and when none can, `notify_person` says on which devices they're turned off; while {{user}} is using Claktory on any device (its window in front), nothing notifies but alerts, since what waits shows in the app. An agent notifies at most 6 times an hour; past that, one more notification says how many more it sent. An agent's alerts replace its earlier alert rather than piling up, and so do its replies.
- **Alerts wait in Needs you:** every alert, an agent's `notify_person` or Claktory's own (its disk can't be written, the machine runs short or a newer model is out with no admin agent to take it), waits in Needs you until {{user}} opens it or taps **Mark as read** (**Mark all 3 as read** on three or more from one sender, folded together), then moves to History, which says when it was read and on which device. It waits whatever became of its notification (held by quiet hours or the hourly cap, alerts or the agent's notifications turned off, no device that shows them), counts once in the badge and in the one notification when quiet hours end, and tapping its notification opens Needs you at it, highlighted (or, read on another device already, its agent's conversation at it). Urgent alerts come first, above every request, marked Urgent in red; the rest follow the requests under **Alerts**, newest first, each a light row with who sent it, when, and its title and words whole. Clicking one opens its agent's conversation at it. Only {{user}} reads one: no agent can clear it. One from an agent that had read a web page or another Claktory's words shows only who sent it, with **Open conversation**, as its notification does. Alerts aren't questions: they never count toward an agent's waiting requests.
- An agent that has read a web page (or is limited to a pipe) never puts its own words in a notification: it says only "Kai has something for you", since the words may be someone else's. A pipe message held for review never says what it says, nor any name the other side chose: "A message in Website ⇄ Marketing waits for your review" when {{user}} named the pipe, otherwise "A message in a pipe waits for your review".
- **Urgent alerts and alerts about the same thing:** only some agents send urgent alerts: admin agents such as Rivet always, and, unless {{user}} chooses otherwise, every agent that leads at least one other. Another agent's `urgent: true` goes as a normal alert, never refused, and the tool's result says who may ("Sent as a normal alert: only Rivet and Bruno may send urgent ones."). When an agent alerts about the same thing as another agent's alert that's still unread and less than 30 minutes old, it folds into that one instead of arriving as its own: no notification of its own, the earlier alert shows "Also from" with its name, and the agent is told whose alert it folded into. Two alerts are about the same thing when their titles share at least two words and those make at least 60% of their words together (in lower case, without short words, numbers and words such as "after" or "during", and with endings like "-ing" and "-s" set aside); their texts don't count, so "it's down" and "it's back" stay apart. An agent's own alerts never fold into each other, and nothing folds into or from an alert that shows only who sent it. An urgent alert, from an agent that may send them, folding into one that isn't urgent makes it urgent and notifies once.
- The settings, kept in Claktory's database (`factory.db`, not the settings file, so a Claktory rolled back to an older version still starts): `needs_you`, `alerts`, `replies` (on unless changed) and `desktop_replies` (off), each kind on or off; `preview`, what a notification says: `full` (who, and what it says), `who` ("Pericolo needs you") or `none` ("Claktory has something for you"); quiet hours, `quiet_hours` with `quiet_start` and `quiet_end` in minutes after midnight in the time zone of the computer Claktory runs on, which the app is told (22:00 to 7:00 unless changed), when nothing notifies but urgent alerts if `urgent_breaks_quiet`, and Needs-you items wait: when quiet hours end (or are turned off), those that came during them and still wait notify once, together ("2 things need you", "They came during your quiet hours."; a tap opens Needs you); `relay`, the push relay devices register with (empty for Claktory's own); `agents`, each agent's "Notify me" by id: `always` (every kind, even those turned off) or `never`; and who may send urgent alerts, the default or {{user}}'s own list of agents (kept in a row of its own).
- **In the app**, Settings → Notifications sets them all but the relay: "Notify me when" (An agent needs you, An agent alerts you, An agent replies to you, and Also replies to messages sent from a computer), "Show in notifications" (Who and what it says, Only who, Nothing), Quiet hours with From, To (half hours, in the Claktory's time zone, named when it isn't the device's) and Let urgent alerts through, and **Urgent alerts from**: Rivet and agents that lead others, or agents {{user}} picks (admin agents always), with a way back to the default. Only {{user}} changes it: the agents' settings tool doesn't. Each change sends only what changed, so changes made on two devices at once both stay. The device's own row ("This Mac", "This iPhone", "This iPad") says whether it gets them: Turn on asks the system the first time, and when they're off in the system's settings, Open System Settings (or Open Settings) goes there; back in the app it reads again. On Android it says "This phone doesn't get notifications yet. Open Claktory to see what's new." (or "This tablet…"): Android doesn't show notifications yet, though the settings still apply to {{user}}'s other devices. Send a test notification sends one to every device that gets them, even in quiet hours and with the app in front, saying what a real one will under the chosen preview, and then names where it went ("Sent to this Mac and Ana's iPhone.": the device tapped on when it shows them, iPhones and iPads registered for pushes, Macs with Claktory open), or says no device gets them yet. Each agent's settings have **Notify me**: Default (as Settings → Notifications says), Always or Never.
- **On a Mac**, while the app is open (even in the background), each Claktory it's connected to notifies as the Mac's own notification, unless {{user}} is at that Claktory in the window right now. An urgent alert shows as time-sensitive. Clicking one brings the window forward at what it's about: an agent's request in its conversation, a pipe's held message, a chat. macOS asks once whether Claktory may notify; System Settings → Notifications → Claktory changes it. Only the Claktory the window shows counts as in use, and only while the window is in front, on screen and the Mac awake and unlocked.
- **On iPhone**, Claktory asks once whether it may notify: as a Claktory {{user}} has just paired the phone with lets it in, or, for a phone paired before, the first time they send something from it (never at launch); Settings → Notifications → Claktory changes it. From then on the iPhone registers with each Claktory it connects to, and its notifications show even when Claktory is closed or the phone is locked: the phone opens each one itself with the key it keeps for that Claktory, under the Claktory's name, and one it can't open, or an old or replayed one, says only "Claktory has something for you". An urgent alert shows as time-sensitive. Tapping one opens what it's about, like a click on a Mac. The badge clears when the app opens, and removing a Claktory from the phone forgets its key.
- **Phones:** a phone that's not connected gets notifications pushed through Claktory's push relay, since only Claktory's own key can push to its app through Apple (Android phones don't get notifications yet); a Claktory run elsewhere uses it too. The phone registers with the relay, which gives it a handle, makes a key, and gives both to Claktory, which keeps them in `{{home}}/remote/notifications.json` (only this user can read it; removing the device forgets them). Each notification is sealed with the phone's key before it leaves: the relay and Apple see only ciphertext, padded to a few fixed sizes, with a counter and the time inside so the phone shows a replayed one only as "Claktory has something for you". Nothing is pushed to a device whose notifications are turned off in its Settings, or while its app is in front, or while {{user}} is using Claktory on another device that's still connected or was in the last 2 minutes; a phone whose app just went to the background gets it, since its app can't show it any more. What the relay learns: each phone's push token and handle, the address a phone registers from and the address each Claktory sends from (so it could link a Claktory's address to its phones), when, and how urgent; never what a notification says, or whose it is. Someone who stole Claktory's home folder or a backup could send readable notifications to the phones registered in it until they register again.

<!-- admin: remote-devices | Using this Claktory from another machine: the private tunnel, adding and removing devices, relays, what runs where. -->
## Claktory on another machine

Claktory's backend can run on an always-on machine (a Mac mini, a Linux server) and serve the app on other devices, so agents keep working while the laptop sleeps or travels. Every device shows the same Claktory.
- Claktory reaches other devices through its **private tunnel** (iroh): every connection is encrypted end to end between Claktory's key and the device's, and goes directly between the two machines when it can (the same network, or a path punched through the routers), or else through a relay that passes it on without being able to read it. A device whose direct connection gets no answer back (a machine on two networks can answer from the wrong one) switches to the relay within a few seconds, and goes direct again only on a path checked both ways. On a weak signal, a connection still bringing data is kept as long as data keeps arriving (one where nothing arrives for about 10 seconds is dropped and made again), and a try to connect that runs out of time gives the next one longer, up to 30 seconds, until one connects. Claktory opens no port to the internet and asks the router for none; a device that isn't paired is turned away right after the encrypted handshake, before it sees anything of Claktory. It works from any network, with no VPN, account or port to set up. Access is on while a device is paired, or its code is waiting: adding a device turns it on and removing the last one turns it off. While Claktory is signed in to claktory.com, the relays are Claktory's own (`relay.claktory.com`), which it shows a token from its account so the relay knows it, and, in case that's down, n0's US East one (iroh's makers); while it isn't signed in (or is signed in to an account service of {{user}}'s own), n0's public relays, since Claktory's relay carries traffic only to or from a signed-in Claktory. The settings file can name others (`remote.relays`). Claktory uses whichever of them it reaches fastest, and its devices follow it: each uses the relays its Claktory names. With your own, connections travel only your relays. Either way Claktory publishes where it can be found (its key, relay and addresses, never data) through iroh's public address lookup, so devices paired before a relay change keep finding it, those paired while n0's relays were the default too. Devices only look Claktory up there; they publish nothing about themselves. On a Mac, macOS asks once whether Claktory Agents (Claktory's backend) may use the Local Network, saying why, and asks the same of the Claktory app when it connects to a Claktory on another machine; denied, devices on the same network connect through a relay instead, and Settings → Devices on a Claktory running on a Mac says where to turn it on (System Settings → Privacy & Security → Local Network).
- Pair each device: in the app showing that Claktory, Settings → Devices → **Add a device…** makes its code, shown once as a QR code with Copy code (or ask Rivet, which starts it for {{user}}: the code shows in a request in its conversation; see the Devices extension); the list there shows when each device last connected, and Remove takes one's access away at once, closing its connection. Or on that machine: `{{home}}/bin/factoryd pair [<device name>]` prints a code (without a name, the device that uses it gives its own, such as "Matt's MacBook"), `factory://?t=…&name=…&e=…` (`e=` is Claktory's key; `r=` names a relay and `a=` an address, when there are any; the apps read the same code written `claktory://?t=…` too, every way it comes in), once, and in a terminal as a QR code too. It holds the device's token, so treat it like a password, though a code alone lets no one in: the first device to use it waits, showing four digits, until {{user}} allows it on a device that's already in, by typing the four digits it shows (the device that's in shows it as calling itself "<name>", with Allow and Don't allow; Claktory checks the digits, and a third wrong number turns the device away), and any other device is turned away. A device waiting to be allowed is in Needs you on every device, and notifies once ("A device is waiting to be allowed on <this Claktory>.", never its name, and at most 3 an hour); the notification can't allow it. If you didn't just add a device yourself, don't allow it. A device that copied the code shows different digits. Don't allow ends the code and forgets that device; make a new code to try again. Cancel on the waiting device takes its request back at once: it leaves Needs you, and the code waits again for a device to use it while it lasts (one that came through the account is forgotten). A code lasts only while {{user}} pairs: closing Add a device ends it, and so do its 10 minutes running out; nothing asks to be allowed otherwise. `factoryd pair` in a terminal waits for the device itself and asks there for the four digits its screen shows (none, or three wrong, turn it away; Ctrl-C ends the code); `factoryd allow <device> --number <digits>` and `factoryd deny <device>` answer a device waiting from elsewhere. `factoryd devices` lists paired devices and when each last connected, and a waiting one's digits; `factoryd unpair <device>` takes one's access away at once. Only the person, or an agent on that machine, can run these.
- With an account: while this Claktory is signed in to claktory.com (Settings → Account), a device signed in to the same account finds it there and connects without a code, proving the account with a short-lived note from claktory.com for that device's key. The first such device, within 30 minutes of this Claktory signing in and while no other device has been added (a new server's case), is in at once; any other waits for Allow with its four digits typed, as with a code. A code used on a device signed in to the same account needs no Allow. While signed in, access from other devices stays on so devices can find it. A device signed in to another account is turned away, before Allow and after (a Claktory is one person's: to work with someone else's agents, open a pipe), and so is one whose sign-in can't be checked. A device added with a code is {{user}}'s once {{user}} allows it with its four digits on this Mac's app, on a device that's confirmed itself, or at a terminal (`factoryd pair`, or `factoryd allow` in one), with no deadline. One let in otherwise, by a program (`factoryd allow` with no terminal) or from a device not confirmed itself, has to confirm before it connects while this Claktory is signed in: signing in on the device confirms it (and so does signing this Claktory in to the account from that device, with the code typed there), or **Confirm it's yours** for it in Settings → Devices on a device that's confirmed already. Devices added before Allows were recorded count as allowed. Each Allow is kept with the device (when, and by which device, the terminal or a program; never the digits). A device found through the account stays connected only while this Claktory is signed in to that account: signing it out, its removal at claktory.com (seen at its next check-in), switching it to another account or turning signing in off closes those connections at once (after the answer to the request that did it), each told why as if it connected again; devices added with a code, and this Mac's app, stay connected.
- Setting Claktory up from a shell, by {{user}} or an agent on that machine (on a server over SSH, say): `factoryd status`, `factoryd account sign-in --email <email>` then `factoryd account verify --code <code>` (the code from {{user}}'s email; `account status`, `resend`, `sign-out`), `factoryd settings set <key> <value>` (`user.name`, `name`, `color`, `default_model`, `default_effort`, checked as the app checks them), `factoryd pair [<name>] --no-wait` (the code at once, without waiting for the device; without a name, the device that uses it gives its own, such as "Matt's MacBook"), `factoryd provider key <provider> --stdin` (an API key piped in, never in the command; `--label` names it) `factoryd login <provider> --json` (a browser sign-in's address, then the code or address pasted on stdin) and `factoryd receive --json` (moving another computer's Claktory here: {{user}} compares the digits; see Moving), with `devices` (a waiting device, never its digits, and with `--json` its key, `endpoint_id`, for a setup on that device to compare with its own), `allow` (`--number <digits>`, always needed: the four digits {{user}} reads on the device's own screen, which Claktory checks, three wrong turning the device away; left out at a terminal, it asks for them; run by a program rather than {{user}} at a terminal, with `--json` or without, it lets in only the first device, and once one is in it refuses (`has_devices`): a device that's in allows the next, in Settings → Devices, or {{user}} runs `factoryd allow <device>` in a terminal on that machine, over SSH with `ssh -t`, since without it there's no terminal), `deny` and `unpair`; `--help` on any of them says how to use it. Each talks to the running Claktory with this machine's `local-pairing` token, so it decides as the app would. With `--json` each prints one JSON line, `{"ok":true,…}` or `{"ok":false,"error":…,"message":…}`, and exits 0 when done, 2 when the next step is {{user}}'s (reading the emailed code, opening a pairing code on the device), 3 when refused and 4 when Claktory isn't running; `factoryd help` lists them. `pair`, `allow`, `deny`, `unpair`, `devices` and `login` without `--json` (or `--no-wait`) work as before.
- On an iPhone: open Claktory and tap **Scan code instead** (or **Paste code instead**), or point the Camera app at the code, which opens Claktory with it. On a Mac: click **+** at the bottom of the rail at the window's far left, then **Use a code instead**, paste the code and click Connect. While it waits to be allowed, the app shows the four digits to type there (on that Claktory, or on another device that uses it) and, under **No screen on <machine>?**, for a machine with no screen (a server), the command to run there, `factoryd allow <device id> --number <digits>`, with Copy. On a Mac, a link does the same: `open "claktory://add?code=<the code, URL-encoded>"` (from a page) asks "Connect to this Claktory?" and connects only on Connect. Every way in (a scan, a link or a pasted code, on iPhone too) asks first, showing the Claktory's fingerprint and the name it gives itself, and warns when that Claktory isn't on {{user}}'s Claktory account (known only while the device is signed in and the account's list could be read; otherwise it only cautions to connect one {{user}} is setting up right now). The device remembers it, and the rail switches back to **This Mac**, or to another Claktory.
- When a Claktory can't be used, a page says so in place of its content (a phone's screen), with what to do there: **Can't connect to <name>** (make sure it's on, awake and online; it keeps trying) with **Details** (the connection in full) and **Try again**, and on an iPhone or iPad "Is Local Network on?" with **Check Local Network**; **<name> no longer allows this Mac** (removed there) or, when this device hasn't seen it let in since the app opened, **<name> doesn't allow this Mac**, with how to get a new code (`factoryd pair` there, with Copy, when this device knows no other device of yours is connected to it; otherwise its Settings → Devices), **Remove…** and **Use a new code** (Add a Claktory at its code; on a phone, its own Add a Claktory page with Back to the Claktory); **<name> was signed out**, saying to sign it in again in its Settings → Account or, with no screen, to connect with a code from `factoryd pair` there (with Copy) and sign it in from here, with **Try again**; waiting to be allowed, another account and signing in to connect, as above. On a phone the way on is a full-width button and the ways out links under it. An iPhone or iPad connecting to its first Claktory says first why Claktory asks for Local Network (so it connects directly on the same Wi-Fi, which is faster), and its **Continue** brings iOS's own prompt; declined, it says Claktory still connects through Claktory's servers (its relay), more slowly on the same Wi-Fi and not at all where that Wi-Fi has no internet, with **Open Settings** (the app's page there, where its Local Network switch is) and **Not now**, which connects all the same, and it carries on by itself once Local Network is turned on. Its other permissions work the same way: before iOS (or Android) first asks for the camera (the scan screen itself says why, with **Continue**), the microphone (a small popover over the mic, the first time it's tapped) or notifications (right after the first pairing), Claktory says why in a sheet (on an iPad, a dialog in the middle of the window, at every size) with **Continue** only; declined, it says what that stops and what still works, with **Open Settings** and **Not now** (for the camera, **Paste code instead**), and closes by itself once it's turned on. One that the device's restrictions keep off (Screen Time, a work profile) says so, with only **Not now**.
- An agent setting Claktory up on a server and, with a shell there too, on a Mac where Claktory's app has no Claktory yet (after {{user}} ran a setup prompt that says so) connects the Mac with no one pasting or reading digits: it writes `{"code": "<the code>"}` to `~/Library/Application Support/dev.factory.app/connect-request.json`, readable only by that user (`umask 077`), written first under another name in the same folder and then moved into place with `mv`, so the app never reads it half-written, and runs `open -g -b dev.factory.app`. The app takes the file at once (removing it, whatever it says), connects, and writes how it goes beside it in `connect-status.json`: `{"state":"connecting"}`, then `{"state":"waiting","device_id","number","endpoint_id"}`, `{"state":"connected","name"}`, or `{"state":"failed","reason"}`. The agent allows it on the server only once `factoryd devices --json` shows that device (the one its code made) waiting with the same `endpoint_id` as the status file: `factoryd allow <device id> --number <the status file's number>`, the digits the Mac's own app wrote, never read from the server. The app then tells {{user}}, once, that a setup on this Mac connected it. A Mac that has any Claktory saved refuses (`already_connected`), as do a request file that's a link, someone else's, readable by others, older than 10 minutes or dated ahead; links (`claktory://add`) always ask. On a Claktory that already has devices, `factoryd allow` from a program is refused (`has_devices`): new devices are allowed from one of them, or in a terminal.
- This machine's own app connects through the tunnel too, on this machine only, with a code Claktory writes to `{{home}}/remote/local-pairing` (only that user can read it: Claktory keeps `{{home}}` and `remote/` open to that user alone); access turning on or off never disconnects it. The local port (7420) wants the token from `local-pairing` too, always, so only that user's programs drive Claktory (scripts can use it); the app falls back to it when the tunnel can't start. Devices paired before the tunnel can't connect: remove them and add them again.
- The agents run on that machine: projects, git credentials, tools and sign-ins live there, and a project folder's path is one of its paths: in project settings, type it, or click Choose… to browse that machine's folders (click a folder to open it; Up, Home and the path at the top move around; Show hidden shows folders starting with "."; Choose takes the folder you're in). Keep it awake. Sign-ins finish from the device you're using: when the app shows a Claktory on another machine and opens an address that comes back to `localhost` (a provider's sign-in in Settings → Providers, or a link clicked in a conversation, such as one an agent's command printed), it passes that port's traffic on to the same port on Claktory's machine, through the tunnel, before the browser opens. A sign-in's stops when it ends; a link's after 10 minutes, or with Stop in the sidebar's footer, which shows "Waiting for the browser…" and then "Passed on to <machine>". The app says so when another app on the device uses that port, nothing on Claktory's machine listens there, or that Claktory is too old to take it (update it); pasting the address the browser ends on still works then. Only paired devices can, and not to Claktory's own port. On an iPhone the page opens in the system's sign-in sheet, so the app stays running. Web searches come from its address.
- A device elsewhere updates with the same Update banner: Claktory's backend updates first, then its app fetches the new app from that Claktory over the same encrypted connection (on a Linux machine, Claktory downloads the Mac release for it first), in pieces sized to the connection and carrying on after a drop, checks it, replaces itself and reopens. A release made before this, or one with no app, says why it can't update the app; the backend can still be updated.
- On Linux (x86_64 or aarch64, any distribution with systemd, NixOS included), Claktory runs as its backend alone, a single static program, with the app on a Mac or an iPhone. The same online installer as a Mac's, `curl -fsSL <channel>/install.sh | bash`, run as the person (not root), installs it as a systemd user service (`~/.config/systemd/user/factoryd.service`) that starts at boot, and shows a code for the first device, also as a QR code drawn in the terminal: scan it with Claktory on an iPhone, or on a Mac click Add a Claktory (+) at the far left of the window and paste it, then allow the device in the terminal when it asks. `factoryd pair` makes a code for another device later, the same way: the installer puts `factoryd` on PATH (a link in `~/.local/bin`, or `/usr/local/bin` for root; on a Mac too) and says where. `bash -s -- --yes` installs without the code and without waiting; `--json`, for an agent, also prints one JSON line (`{"ok":true,…}` with `factoryd status`'s answer, or `{"ok":false,"error":…,"message":…}`) and exits 0 when done, 3 when this machine can't run it, 4 when it's installed but not running (`port_in_use`: another user's Claktory on this computer has its port). On a Mac, `--app-only` installs only the app, from the .dmg, for a Claktory elsewhere. Starting at boot needs lingering (`loginctl enable-linger`), which the installer turns on when it may, and otherwise says to run `sudo loginctl enable-linger <user>`; on NixOS the configuration's `users.users.<name>.linger = true;` does it, and agents need `git` added to the packages. No port needs opening in its firewall. Updates come as on a Mac: the backend downloads them and the connected app offers them. Its log is `{{home}}/factoryd.log`. On Windows, it runs in WSL 2 (the note on Claktory on Windows).

<!-- admin: wsl | Claktory on Windows through WSL 2: what's supported, the installer's checks, the keep-running task, and what agents never do to the Windows host. -->
## Claktory on Windows (WSL 2)

On Windows, Claktory runs in WSL 2, as on any Linux machine (Claktory on another machine), with the app on a Mac, an iPhone or an Android phone; there's no Windows app or native Windows Claktory. The supported setup: Windows 11, WSL 2 with systemd on, Ubuntu 24.04, a normal Linux user whose home is in that Linux (not on a Windows drive), and the Linux's ordinary disk, never a sparse one.
- The installer (the same `curl -fsSL <channel>/install.sh | bash`, run in that Linux) checks first, before changing anything, with `claktory-wsl check`: WSL 2 rather than WSL 1, systemd running, lingering on (the check turns it on when it may, its one change, or says to run `sudo loginctl enable-linger <user>`), the home folder writable and in Linux, at least 2 GB free in Linux, and, by asking Windows (as the same Windows user, nothing elevated), at least 5 GB free on the Windows drive that holds the Linux's disk and that disk not set as sparse. Linux's own free space shows the disk's ceiling, often 1 TB, not what the Windows drive has left. Each unsafe state stops it with one sentence saying what to do; what it couldn't ask Windows is said to be unverified, never safe.
- Lingering starts Claktory whenever that Linux starts, but Windows stops the Linux a few seconds after its last window closes. One Windows task keeps it running while the person is signed in to Windows: "\Claktory\Keep <distribution> running", which starts the distribution as that Linux user at the person's Windows sign-in, with no administrator rights, no stored password, no wake timer and no time limit, on battery too. The installer offers it in the terminal, naming all that and how to remove it, and adds it only when the person types yes there; with `--yes` or `--json` (an agent's install) it only says how. `claktory-wsl keep-running` shows whether it's on; `claktory-wsl keep-running on` asks the same way (with no terminal it refuses), and `off` removes it. That prompt is for {{user}} at a terminal, not a lock: agents never add it or any other host task themselves (The computer itself). While it runs, the task also keeps Windows from going to sleep on its own when idle (plugged in or on battery), and the question says so; the screen still turns off as set, and closing the lid, the power button or Sleep still put the computer to sleep. `off` (or End, then Delete, in Task Scheduler's Claktory folder) ends both. While Windows sleeps or is off, Claktory stops, and it carries on when Windows wakes and the person is signed in. `claktory-wsl check` also notes when Windows sleeps on its own while plugged in and the task is off, when Windows doesn't sign the person in after an update restarts it (Settings → Accounts → Sign-in options → "Use my sign-in info to automatically finish setting up after an update"), and when Claktory's service file is from an older install (installing again refreshes it). Updates put `claktory-wsl` in `{{home}}/bin` too, so an install from before it can run `~/.factory/bin/claktory-wsl keep-running on` after updating.
- Agents never tune WSL or Windows (The computer itself): no sparse disks or `--allow-unsafe`, no restarting or stopping WSL, no compacting or repairing its disk, no power, graphics or firewall changes, no other startup tasks or services, and no sudo, even to make Claktory work; they suggest the change, with its risks, and {{user}} decides. On a read-only disk, checksum errors or repeated I/O errors in that Linux, they stop changing things, keep what shows the error, and ask {{user}} to repair or restore the disk from Windows; never remount it writable, run fsck on it while mounted, or reset pairings to hide it.

<!-- admin: diagnostics | Advanced diagnostics: what devices and Claktory record when on (connections first), kept in factory.db for agents to read. -->
## Advanced diagnostics

When something goes wrong that's hard to see from here, such as a device that can't connect or stays on "Reconnecting…", advanced diagnostics show what happened. They're off unless {{user}} turns them on in Settings, for each Claktory separately, or asks Rivet to.
- An admin agent such as Rivet turns them on or off with the `settings` tool (Settings extension; `diagnostics`: true or false), as Settings does, and the tool's answer says whether they're on and what's kept for each source. It turns them on when {{user}} asks, and off only when {{user}} explicitly asks, since that deletes everything kept.
- While they're on, each of {{user}}'s apps records what happens in the areas Claktory knows trouble in, and Claktory records its own side. The first area is `connection`. Apps record launches, going to the background, network changes, each connection attempt and how it went (directly or through a relay, how long it took, the error), and disconnections. Claktory records each device it lets in (`connected`, with how it came: `direct`, `relay`, `this_computer` or `port`), each it turns away (`turned_away`, through the tunnel or on the local port without this computer's token; those alike are a row per 10 seconds with their count) and each disconnection, with how it ended, how long it lasted and what it sent the device, by kind, before compression (`frames=patch:120,delta:900 bytes=patch:4100,delta:90000`; kinds `workspace`, `patch`, `entry`, `delta`, `transcript`, `file`, `other`). Nothing records messages, names, addresses or codes; devices appear by their id. An app sends what it recorded when it connects, then every 30 seconds while connected. Claktory takes it only from this computer's own app and {{user}}'s paired devices.
- An app's `connection` events (the iPhone, iPad, Android and Mac apps), by `kind`: `lifecycle` (`app=started|background|back`, `away_s` when back), `network` (`net=wifi|cellular|wired|other|none`), `connect` (a try: `attempt`, `route=local|tunnel|plain`), `connected` (`attempt`, `route=direct|relayed|tunnel|local|plain`, duration from the try's start), `disconnected` (`attempt`, `why=timeout|refused|unreachable|tunnel|closed|handshake|signin|abandoned|other`, `welcomed=1|0`, the WebSocket close `code`, duration since it connected or tried), `relay` (`up=1|0`, whether the device reaches its relay; up, with how long it took to come up since the tunnel started or the relay was lost as its duration; down, `err=unchosen|dns|timeout|tls|network|other`, roughly why (`unchosen`: no relay answered the device's last measurement of the network), again whenever that changes), `path` (the tunnel's `route` changed) and `reconnect` (`reason=resume|network|asked|watch|check|dropped|runtime|tunnel|patch`: back from the background, the network moved, the person tapped Reconnecting…, not back within the watch's time, a check unanswered, a live connection dropped, the tunnels' runtime didn't answer, the tunnel didn't bring it back, a snapshot that didn't fit). The app remembers on the device whether they're on, so one that can't connect still records why, and sends it once it can. An iPhone's or iPad's `notifications` events say how setting up its notifications with this Claktory went: `registered` (`fresh=1|0`, whether it asked the push relay for a new handle) or `failed` (`step=relay|key|registration|claktory|other`, and for the keychain its `status`, an OSStatus without its minus sign: 34018 means the app isn't entitled to its keychain group).
- It's kept in factory.db. `diagnostics` has one row per event: `source` (`device:` and the paired device's id, `device:local` for this computer's app, or `backend` for Claktory itself), `area`, `session` (goes up each time the app or Claktory starts, so a force-quit or relaunch shows), `seq`, `at` (by the source's clock), `kind`, `detail` (`key=value` tokens), `duration` in ms, `app_version` and `received`. `diagnostics_sources` sums it up per source (a device's name, kind, system and app version as it last sent, how many events and over what time) and `diagnostics_areas` per source and area. Agents with Claktory's records (`database:factory`) read them with SQL: Rivet, and any agent {{user}} gives them to.
- Each source keeps 14 days or 4 MB, whichever is less, and the oldest go first. Settings shows what's kept for each source and area, and can delete one source's, one area's or all of it. Turning diagnostics off deletes everything kept, and recording stops.

<!-- admin: moving | Moving this Claktory to another computer: in Settings, factoryd move and receive, or export and import; passphrase, paths, --restore. -->
## Moving to another computer

Claktory moves to another computer (a new Mac, a Linux server) with everything in it: agents and their conversations, records, settings, secrets, sign-ins, paired devices and the projects' folders. It's done in the app (Settings → General → **Move to another computer…**), or from a shell, with `factoryd move` or `factoryd export` on this computer and `factoryd receive` or `factoryd import` on the other.
- `factoryd export --final --out FILE` stops Claktory here and writes it into one encrypted file, a **bundle**, then prints its **passphrase** once: 32 letters and digits in groups of four. Write it down: the bundle can't be opened without it, and it isn't kept anywhere. Run it in Terminal, not from an agent: it stops the Claktory the agent runs in.
- The bundle holds the projects' folders too: a git repository as its history (every branch, agents' included), its uncommitted changes and the files git doesn't ignore; build output (`target/`, `node_modules`, anything git ignores) and agents' checkouts stay behind and are made again there, and so do the image previews Claktory keeps for phones (`{{home}}/cache`), made again when asked. `--skip FOLDER` leaves a folder out (one already on the other computer, say).
- Before anything stops, it checks there's room for the bundle where it goes, enough memory, and git; if not, it says why and changes nothing. If anything fails after Claktory stopped, it starts again as it was.
- Afterwards this copy is kept but can't run: its sign-ins, secrets, account and devices' keys are put away in `{{home}}/moved/`, and Claktory doesn't start here at login. If the move doesn't work out, `factoryd export --restore` brings it back as it was. Once the moved Claktory works, `factoryd export --delete-moved` deletes this copy for good (the workspace stays); both ask {{user}} to type a word to go ahead. On a Mac, the app here shows only where Claktory went and when (the rail stays, to reach another Claktory), with **Delete this copy…**, which does the same once {{user}} types "delete"; it then says when the copy was deleted. Other devices just follow it to the new computer.
- **Over the network, with no file to carry:** `factoryd receive` on the new computer shows a code; `factoryd move CODE` in Terminal here moves Claktory there. Both computers show six digits, and {{user}} checks they're the same on each before anything is sent. It copies Claktory while its agents keep working, a few rounds until little changes, then stops it for a last round, waits for the new computer to set it up and check it, and only then puts this copy away as `--final` does. If anything fails before that, nothing moved and Claktory runs here again as it was. A dropped connection is dialed again by itself; while the new computer sets Claktory up, a dropped connection that gets no answer at all for two minutes (its receive stopped, say) ends the move with a message saying so, and Claktory runs here again.
- **In the app**, from any device: Settings → General → **Move to another computer…** shows a line to paste in a terminal on the new computer (it installs Claktory there and runs `factoryd receive`), then takes the code it shows, pasted or, on a device with a camera, scanned from its QR code with **Scan code**; the field says which computer and system the code is from. Then: **They match** once the six digits are the same on both; the plan as the new computer checked it (how much there is to copy, what's in the way, commands the agents use that it lacks, where folders go); a meter while it copies, with **Move now** to pause the agents for the last round at once rather than when the copy has caught up; and **Cancel** until the switch. Closed, the form keeps going; Settings shows how it's going, with **Show progress…**. A move an agent started (Rivet, with the Move extension) waits for {{user}} at the digits: every device shows Claktory's own confirm over the window, naming the other computer, its system and the six digits in fixed words, with **Confirm** and **Cancel move**; once confirmed it says it waits for the other computer. Once the agents pause, the Mac Claktory runs on shows only "Moving to …" (the rail stays) until it's moved, then "Moved to …"; other devices show the same and follow it there by themselves; one that loses its connection at the hand-over says so in place of the last step, without **Cancel move**. A move that fails says why, and Claktory carries on here.
- Without `--final`, `factoryd export` writes a bundle of Claktory as it runs and changes nothing: a rehearsal, or a backup. Import refuses such a bundle unless given `--same-identity`: its Claktory still runs, and the copy would share its identity (the tunnel key, so paired devices could reach either copy, its sign-ins and account). Keep only one of them running.
- On the new computer, install Claktory, then `factoryd import BUNDLE` (it asks for the passphrase; `--passphrase-stdin` reads it from stdin). Paths under the old home folder move to this user's home, and Claktory's own folder to this one's. `--map /old/path=/new/path` puts a folder somewhere else; a folder outside the home folder needs one. A move over the network needs none: a folder outside the old home folder (on another disk, or in `/opt`) goes under the new user's home, in `moved-from-<old computer>/` with its whole path (`/Volumes/Data/Assets` becomes `~/moved-from-matts-mac/Volumes/Data/Assets`), and the plan shows it before copying starts; `factoryd move --skip FOLDER` leaves one out.
- Import makes each project folder again from its git history, with its uncommitted changes and untracked files, but not what git ignores (build output, dependencies). It makes each agent's own checkout again on its branch, with its changes; a patch that doesn't apply is kept beside the checkout. It refuses a newer Claktory's bundle, a Claktory here that already has work in it, a folder that's already there, a disk without room for it, and a home on a network disk.
- It's safe to stop: until import has finished and checked everything, this Claktory doesn't start. Run `factoryd import BUNDLE` again to carry on, or `factoryd import --abandon` to undo it.
- The vault gets a new key on disk, so the old computer's copy no longer opens it. Paired devices follow the Claktory to its new computer without being added again. The old computer's own app becomes an ordinary device: import prints a code to paste into it (Add a Claktory), allowed like any new device.
- Each agent is told once, when Claktory starts: where it is now, how paths changed, its checkout, the background tasks the move stopped (processes don't move), and tools its tasks used that this computer hasn't got. Its conversation keeps the old paths. Build caches don't move, so first builds are slow.
- Signed in to claktory.com, the moved Claktory gets a new token and key there at its first start, so the old computer's copy can't sign in or unlock its secrets any more; the account is emailed that it moved. Its vault and account stay the same.
- On the new computer, `factoryd receive` shows its code as text and a QR code, and asks {{user}} to type yes once the digits match. The moved Claktory starts there only once the old one has handed over. Turning the digits down makes a new code, and only one computer at a time can pair. Stopped, `factoryd receive` carries on when run again, with the same code and what was copied kept; `factoryd import --abandon` undoes it. If the old computer says it moved but the new one is still waiting, `factoryd receive --committed` finishes it there. An agent runs `factoryd receive --json`: one JSON line per step, `{"event":"code","code":…}` (again with a new code after the digits were turned down), `{"event":"progress","message":…}`, and `{"event":"confirm","digits":…,"machine":…}`, when the agent shows {{user}} those digits to compare with the old computer's and writes {{user}}'s answer, yes or no, on its stdin, never answering for them (the old computer asks {{user}} to confirm too); then `{"ok":true,…}`, or `{"ok":false,"error":…,"message":…}` with exit 3 when refused, or called off by the old computer after the digits were confirmed (`"error":"called_off"`: nothing moved, nothing changed here), 1 when it failed, 130 when stopped (Ctrl-C, or a TERM signal, after which it carries on when run again).
- What's in a bundle, how it's sealed, and how the network move goes, is in `docs/MOVE.md` in Claktory's source.

<!-- admin: vault-backup | factoryd vault backup and restore: an encrypted copy of the secrets and what opens them, for a nightly off-site backup and restore drills. -->
## Backing up the vault

- `factoryd vault backup --recipient RECIPIENT > FILE` writes the vault (secrets, provider sign-ins) and what opens it to standard output, encrypted with age to RECIPIENT: an age key (`age1…`) or an SSH public key (`ssh-ed25519 …`); `--recipient` again adds another. What opens it goes along: the key on disk (`auto.key`), or, signed in to claktory.com, this computer's half of the key and the sign-in there (`account/`); a vault in locked mode opens only with its passphrase or recovery key. It runs while Claktory runs and changes nothing; it says on stderr how many secrets it holds and how it opens. The file is age around a tar, so `age -d -i IDENTITY FILE | tar -t` lists it.
- `factoryd vault restore FILE --identity IDENTITY_FILE --home DIR` puts it back into DIR, a fresh home with no vault or account (one that has either is refused). The identity file holds an age identity (`AGE-SECRET-KEY-1…`) or an SSH private key without a passphrase. It checks every file against the backup's list before writing any, then says how the vault opens there, and opens one that opens by itself to check it. `--json` prints one JSON line (`ok`, `message`, `opens`: key, account or locked, `secrets`, `opened`, `created`).
- Either holds everything that opens the secrets: keep the identity off this computer, and the backups where only it reads them.

<!-- admin: models-and-sign-in | Model providers, how to sign in to each (subscriptions, API keys, pasting the redirect), and why only the person can. -->
## Models and signing in

Providers and how to sign in to each, all in Settings → Providers:
